242	
243	Table~\ref{tab:dose-response-detail} reports per-task death rates for Reflexion at $N{=}3$, 4, and 5 on the 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}20$ episodes per task). Per-task variance is high (up to 25\,pp range for \texttt{measure-melting-point}), but the aggregate smooths to a 2.5\,pp band, confirming that the stabilization conclusion is robust at the aggregate level despite task-level noise. \texttt{freeze} is consistently the hardest task (50--60\% DR across all $N$).
244	
245	\paragraph{Statistical power.} With $n{=}200$ paired episodes, the minimum detectable effect (MDE) is ${\sim}8$~pp ($\alpha = 0.05$, 80\% power). The key $N{=}1{\to}3$ decline ($-13.5$~pp) exceeds this threshold and is well-powered; the stabilization claim ($N{=}3{\to}5$, $+1.0$~pp) is also supported, since a ${\geq}8$~pp improvement would be detected if present.

396	\section{Penalty Sensitivity Analysis}
397	\label{app:penalty}
398	
399	\begin{table}[h]
400	\centering
401	\small
402	\begin{tabular}{@{}rrr@{}}
403	\toprule
404	$P$ & DAF($P$) & 95\% CI \\
405	\midrule
406	0 & 7.4\% & [$-39.4$, $82.5$] \\
407	1 & 14.8\% & [$-28.6$, $89.6$] \\
408	5 & 35.4\% & [$-1.8$, $92.7$] \\
409	10 & 50.4\% & [$18.5$, $93.3$] \\
410	25 & 70.7\% & [$48.0$, $96.3$] \\
411	50 & 82.6\% & [$67.2$, $98.2$] \\
412	100\textsuperscript{*} & 90.4\% & [$81.3$, $99.1$] \\
413	200 & 94.9\% & [$89.9$, $99.5$] \\
414	500 & 97.9\% & [$95.7$, $99.8$] \\
415	\bottomrule
416	\end{tabular}
417	\caption{Sensitivity of DAF to death penalty magnitude $P$. DAF$(P) = 1 - \Delta_{\text{nd}} / \Delta_{\text{full}}(P)$, where $\Delta_{\text{full}}(P) \approx \delta \cdot P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}$, $\delta = 0.2653$ is the death-rate differential, and $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$ (strict neither-died filtering). Recomputed from 245 episode-level data; bootstrap 95\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$). \textsuperscript{*}Default setting.}
418	\label{tab:penalty-sensitivity}
419	\end{table}
420	
421	The non-death score gap $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$ is invariant across penalty levels; it depends only on surviving-episode performance, not on $P$. As $P$ increases, $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ grows ($16.33$ at $P{=}50$, $29.60$ at $P{=}100$, $56.13$ at $P{=}200$) while $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ remains fixed, mechanically increasing DAF.
422	
423	\paragraph{Mechanism boundary: death specificity.}
424	The $r = 0.97$ correlation between per-task death-rate differential and $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ is specific to catastrophic death (score $= -100$). Broader failure definitions dissolve the correlation entirely: defining ``failure'' as any non-positive score ($T{=}0$) yields $r = -0.13$ ($p = 0.70$), because non-death episodes show near-zero performance differential between strategies ($\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$). Table~\ref{tab:penalty-correlation} quantifies this specificity across penalty magnitudes.
425	
426	\begin{table}[h]
427	\centering
428	\small
429	\begin{tabular}{@{}rccl@{}}
430	\toprule
431	$P$ & $r$ & $p$ & Sig.\ ($\alpha{=}0.05$) \\
432	\midrule
433	1 & $-0.44$ & $0.178$ & No \\
434	5 & $-0.29$ & $0.382$ & No \\
435	10 & $-0.08$ & $0.813$ & No \\
436	25 & $0.51$ & $0.108$ & No \\
437	50 & $0.86$ & $0.0006$ & Yes \\
438	100\textsuperscript{*} & $0.97$ & $8 \times 10^{-7}$ & Yes \\
439	200 & $0.99$ & $1 \times 10^{-9}$ & Yes \\
440	\bottomrule
441	\end{tabular}
442	\caption{Pearson $r$ between per-task death-rate differential and $\Delta_{\text{full}}(P)$ across penalty magnitudes. The correlation is non-significant at low penalties and emerges at $P \geq 50$, which reflects the transition from non-death-dominated to death-dominated score gaps. \textsuperscript{*}Default ScienceWorld setting.}
443	\label{tab:penalty-correlation}
444	\end{table}
445	
446	This sharpens our central finding: death is not merely the dominant component of routing signal; it is effectively the \emph{only} component that generates differential routing signal in ScienceWorld. At low penalties, the death contribution is small and $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ reduces to $\Delta_{\text{nd}} \approx 2.84$, which is near-uniform across task types ($\text{mean non-death gap} = -1.19$ excluding the \texttt{find-non-living-thing} outlier); consequently, death-rate differential has no predictive power. As $P$ increases, the death term dominates $\Delta_{\text{full}}$, and the correlation sharpens because death rates \emph{are} the performance gap. The DAF values in Table~\ref{tab:penalty-sensitivity} increase monotonically with $P$, but this is a mathematical consequence of the decomposition ($\text{DAF} = \delta P / (\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}})$), not evidence of mechanism robustness.
447

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/label_decomposition.pdf}
7	\caption{Episode-level label decomposition ($N{=}245$). Of 95 non-tie labels, 75 (78.9\%) arise from death asymmetry: 54 where death is the sole discriminator and 21 with co-occurring score differences. All 20 non-death labels originate from a single task type (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}).}
8	\label{fig:label-decomposition}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	Our diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\% confidence intervals for all metrics.
12	
13	\begin{algorithm}[t]
14	\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}
15	\label{alg:labeling}
16	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
17	\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, computation modes $\mathcal{M} = \{{\tt int}, {\tt det}\}$
18	\ENSURE Label $\ell \in \{{\tt det\text{-}pref}, {\tt int\text{-}pref}, {\tt no\text{-}pref}\}$
19	\FOR{each $m \in \mathcal{M}$}
20	    \STATE Run episode $\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$
21	    \STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$
22	\ENDFOR
23	\IF{$d_{\tt int} = 1$ \AND $d_{\tt det} = 0$}
24	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt det\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{death asymmetry}
25	\ELSIF{$d_{\tt int} = 0$ \AND $d_{\tt det} = 1$}
26	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt int\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{death asymmetry}
27	\ELSIF{$d_{\tt int} = d_{\tt det} = 1$}
28	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt no\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{both dead}
29	\ELSIF{$s_{\tt det} > s_{\tt int}$}
30	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt det\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{score comparison}
31	\ELSIF{$s_{\tt int} > s_{\tt det}$}
32	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt int\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{score comparison}
33	\ELSE
34	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt no\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{tied alive}
35	\ENDIF
36	\RETURN $\ell$
37	\end{algorithmic}
38	\end{algorithm}
39	
40	\subsection{Problem Setup}
41	\label{sec:problem-setup}
42	
43	Consider an interactive environment with observation space $\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\pi: \mathcal{O}^* \rightarrow \mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\mathcal{M} = \{{\tt int}, {\tt det}\}$: \textbf{internal} (${\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\citep{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \textbf{deterministic} (${\tt det}$), a privileged-information strategy (an umbrella term: ground-truth simulator access in ScienceWorld, code interpreter in MATH, admissible-action enumeration in ALFWorld---see \S\ref{sec:cross-env} for environment-specific operationalizations) that eliminates observation uncertainty; these qualitative differences affect what routing signals the framework can detect. A \emph{routing policy} $\rho: \mathcal{O}^* \rightarrow \mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\rho$.
44	
45	\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}
46	\label{sec:collection}
47	
48	Per-step oracle labeling degenerates under sparse terminal rewards: on ScienceWorld \citep{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\% of steps (5{,}935 of 6{,}094 across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores, yielding no per-step routing signal. Episode-level comparison---directly measuring terminal outcomes---is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments.
49	
50	\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}
```

91	\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under improvement methods. Top: all 11 tasks. Bottom: 10 death-prone tasks (\texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded). No intervention approaches deterministic-level safety.}
92	\label{tab:agent-improvement}

94	
95	The labeling function is \emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \texttt{no\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Label distributions are reported in \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}.
96	
97	\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}
98	\label{sec:daf}
99	
100	To quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\delta = \mathbb{E}[d^{\tt int}] - \mathbb{E}[d^{\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \bar{s}_{\text{surv}}$, where $\bar{s}_{\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\bar{s}_{\text{surv}} \ll P$ (in our data, $\bar{s}_{\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\approx}\,\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:
101	\begin{equation}
102	    \Delta_{\text{full}} \approx \underbrace{\delta \cdot P}_{\text{death contribution}} + \Delta_{\text{nd}}
103	\end{equation}
104	The approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\%$ of $\Delta_{\text{full}}$).
105	
106	We define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:
107	\begin{equation}
108	    \text{DAF} = 1 - \frac{\Delta_{\text{nd}}}{\Delta_{\text{full}}} \approx \frac{\delta P}{\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}}
109	\end{equation}%
110	\footnote{When $\Delta_{\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}
111	
112	DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}); we treat the label fraction (78.9\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; death rates and $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\citep{ng1999policy}. Uncertainty is quantified via episode-level bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$, percentile 95\% CIs).
113	
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nIn ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone, as the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated), while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \\citep{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \\citep{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\citep{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026}, model tiers \\citep{mtrouter-2026}, or episode-level model selection under sparse rewards \\citep{budget-aware-routing-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. These approaches, which operate in non-catastrophic settings, share the assumption that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---an assumption that remains untested: no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing preferences in interactive environments.\n\nWe propose a \\emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding. Always-deterministic matches 96.3\\% of oracle decisions, capping routing's marginal value at 2.89 points (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}); removing death episodes reveals that oracle access provides almost no non-death advantage ($\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$, 9.6\\% of total gap; \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}). Two independent 70B-class models yield DAF${}=92.4$--$101.0\\%$ (Llama-70B exceeds 100\\%: death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap), and Reflexion ($N{=}3$) similarly drives DAF above 100\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\n\nThis addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\\% tie rate; \\S\\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}). Our approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\citep{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, comparing episode outcomes.\n\nCross-context checks probe specificity: MATH Level~4--5 ($N{=}795$, code interpreter) shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$); Llama-3.1-8B replication ($N{=}220$) confirms death dominance across model families (direction consistency 10/10; \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}). Four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) reduce death rates but none closes the gap to deterministic safety, with ${\\geq}$12~pp residual gaps (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\nAn exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions: (1)~an \\textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\\% tie rate), decomposes routing signal via penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics, and provides build-vs-buy signal from a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot (Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}); (2)~\\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels} in ScienceWorld stem from death asymmetry---replicated across three 7--9B model families (DAF $88$--$90\\%$), four interventions, $10{\\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models (DAF $92.4$--$101.0\\%$), and cross-environment probes (MATH, ALFWorld)---while a step-level router recovers only task-type classification (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\n\n",

    "oldString": "Our diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}).",
    "newString": "Our diagnostic framework (Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}) quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/label_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Episode-level label decomposition ($N{=}245$). Of 95 non-tie labels, 75 (78.9\\%) arise from death asymmetry: 54 where death is the sole discriminator and 21 with co-occurring score differences. All 20 non-death labels originate from a single task type (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing}).}\n\\label{fig:label-decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}\n\\label{alg:labeling}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$\n\\ENSURE Label $\\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}$\n\\FOR{each $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$}\n    \\STATE Run episode $\\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$\n    \\STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\IF{$d_{\\tt int} = 1$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 0$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = 0$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{both dead}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt det} > s_{\\tt int}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt int} > s_{\\tt det}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSE\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{tied alive}\n\\ENDIF\n\\RETURN $\\ell$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment with observation space $\\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic} (${\\tt det}$), a privileged-information strategy (an umbrella term: ground-truth simulator access in ScienceWorld, code interpreter in MATH, admissible-action enumeration in ALFWorld---see \\S\\ref{sec:cross-env} for environment-specific operationalizations) that eliminates observation uncertainty; these qualitative differences affect what routing signals the framework can detect. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$.\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling degenerates under sparse terminal rewards: on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps (5{,}935 of 6{,}094 across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores, yielding no per-step routing signal. Episode-level comparison---directly measuring terminal outcomes---is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and computation mode $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying mode $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM v0.21.0) on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty; complete prompt templates are provided in Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-templates}. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Death\\% & $N$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-trial baseline & 42.9 & 245 \\\\\nReflexion ($3{\\times}$) & 31.4 & 220 \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$) & 30.6 & 255 \\\\\nDeterministic baseline & 16.3 & 245 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\\textit{10 death-prone tasks only:}} \\\\\nDeath-prone baseline & 48.0 & 200 \\\\\nRetry ($5{\\times}$) & 43.5 & 200 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under improvement methods. Top: all 11 tasks. Bottom: 10 death-prone tasks (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded). No intervention approaches deterministic-level safety.}\n\\label{tab:agent-improvement}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \\texttt{no\\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nDAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}); we treat the label fraction (78.9\\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\\citep{ng1999policy}. Uncertainty is quantified via episode-level bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$, percentile 95\\% CIs).\n",

We define two computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic} (${\\tt det}$), a privileged-information strategy (an umbrella term: ground-truth simulator access in ScienceWorld, code interpreter in MATH, admissible-action enumeration in ALFWorld---see \\S\\ref{sec:cross-env} for environment-specific operationalizations) that eliminates observation uncertainty; these qualitative differences affect what routing signals the framework can detect. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$.\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling degenerates under sparse terminal rewards: on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps (5{,}935 of 6{,}094 across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores, yielding no per-step routing signal. Episode-level comparison---directly measuring terminal outcomes---is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and computation mode $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying mode $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM v0.21.0) on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty; complete prompt templates are provided in Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-templates}. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Death\\% & $N$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-trial baseline & 42.9 & 245 \\\\\nReflexion ($3{\\times}$) & 31.4 & 220 \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$) & 30.6 & 255 \\\\\nDeterministic baseline & 16.3 & 245 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\\textit{10 death-prone tasks only:}} \\\\\nDeath-prone baseline & 48.0 & 200 \\\\\nRetry ($5{\\times}$) & 43.5 & 200 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under improvement methods. Top: all 11 tasks. Bottom: 10 death-prone tasks (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded). No intervention approaches deterministic-level safety.}\n\\label{tab:agent-improvement}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \\texttt{no\\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nDAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}); we treat the label fraction (78.9\\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\\citep{ng1999policy}. Uncertainty is quantified via episode-level bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$, percentile 95\\% CIs).\n",

Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM v0.21.0) on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty; complete prompt templates are provided in Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-templates}. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Death\\% & $N$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-trial baseline & 42.9 & 245 \\\\\nReflexion ($3{\\times}$) & 31.4 & 220 \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$) & 30.6 & 255 \\\\\nDeterministic baseline & 16.3 & 245 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\\textit{10 death-prone tasks only:}} \\\\\nDeath-prone baseline & 48.0 & 200 \\\\\nRetry ($5{\\times}$) & 43.5 & 200 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under improvement methods. Top: all 11 tasks. Bottom: 10 death-prone tasks (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded). No intervention approaches deterministic-level safety.}\n\\label{tab:agent-improvement}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \\texttt{no\\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nDAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}); we treat the label fraction (78.9\\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\\citep{ng1999policy}. Uncertainty is quantified via episode-level bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$, percentile 95\\% CIs).\n",

[Director 指令] [Patch] 用户要求重新分布正文(P1-P8) float，规则：
1. 除 P1 外，P2-P8 每页至少 1 个图或表
2. 所有 float 用 `[t]` 置顶
3. 同页多 float 左右双栏均分，不挤一栏

**当前问题**（基于最新编译 22 页 PDF）：
- P2: 0 float ❌（全文本：Intro 续 + Related Work）
- P3: 3 float（Fig1 左 + Algo1 右 + Fig2 左）→ 左栏 2 右栏 1，偏重
- P5: 4 float（Tab2 左 + Tab3 右 + Fig3 左 + Fig4 右）→ 过于拥挤
- P8: 0 float ❌（Discussion + Conclusion + Limitations + Ethics）

**建议策略**（仅供参考，你根据实际 LaTeX 排版效果调整）：

1. **P2 补 float**：将 Figure 2（pipeline overview, `fig:pipeline`）从 method.tex §3.1 移到 introduction.tex 末尾或 related_work.tex 开头，改为 `figure*[t]`（双栏置顶）。Pipeline 概览图放在 Intro→Method 过渡处概念上合理，且双栏图能有效填充 P2 顶部。注意：method.tex:11 已有 `Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline}` 引用，确保引用在图之后。

2. **P8 补 float**：考虑将 Table 6（baselines, `tab:baselines`）的定义在源码中后移，让 LaTeX 自然将其排到 P8 顶部（Table 6 在 §4.5 Baselines 被引用，Discussion 在 §5，距离 ~1 页可接受）。或者从 P5/P6 拥挤页移一个表到更晚位置。

3. **P5 减负**：将 Figure 4（death timeline）或 Table 3（per-task decomposition）的定义后移，让其自然落到 P6。

4. **双栏均衡**：同页 2 个 float 时，一个放左栏一个放右栏（通过调整 float 定义在源码中的顺序和位置）。

**硬约束**：
- 正文仍保持 ~8 页
- 不改动图表内容，只调整 float 位置
- 编译无 warning
- 引用不能出现在图/表之前（forward reference 可接受 1 页内）

完成后编译，逐页截图验证 float 分布，汇报每页 float 清单。

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/label_decomposition.pdf}
7	\caption{Episode-level label decomposition ($N{=}245$). Of 95 non-tie labels, 75 (78.9\%) arise from death asymmetry: 54 where death is the sole discriminator and 21 with co-occurring score differences. All 20 non-death labels originate from a single task type (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}).}
8	\label{fig:label-decomposition}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	Our diagnostic framework (Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline}) quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\% confidence intervals for all metrics.
12	
13	\begin{algorithm}[t]
14	\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}
15	\label{alg:labeling}
16	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
17	\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, computation modes $\mathcal{M} = \{{\tt int}, {\tt det}\}$
18	\ENSURE Label $\ell \in \{{\tt det\text{-}pref}, {\tt int\text{-}pref}, {\tt no\text{-}pref}\}$
19	\FOR{each $m \in \mathcal{M}$}
20	    \STATE Run episode $\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$
21	    \STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$
22	\ENDFOR
23	\IF{$d_{\tt int} = 1$ \AND $d_{\tt det} = 0$}
24	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt det\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{death asymmetry}
25	\ELSIF{$d_{\tt int} = 0$ \AND $d_{\tt det} = 1$}
26	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt int\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{death asymmetry}
27	\ELSIF{$d_{\tt int} = d_{\tt det} = 1$}
28	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt no\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{both dead}
29	\ELSIF{$s_{\tt det} > s_{\tt int}$}
30	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt det\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{score comparison}
31	\ELSIF{$s_{\tt int} > s_{\tt det}$}
32	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt int\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{score comparison}
33	\ELSE
34	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt no\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{tied alive}
35	\ENDIF
36	\RETURN $\ell$
37	\end{algorithmic}
38	\end{algorithm}
39	
40	\subsection{Problem Setup}
41	\label{sec:problem-setup}
42	
43	\begin{figure}[t]
44	\centering
45	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}
46	\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}.}
47	\label{fig:pipeline}
48	\end{figure}
49	
50	Consider an interactive environment with observation space $\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\pi: \mathcal{O}^* \rightarrow \mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\mathcal{M} = \{{\tt int}, {\tt det}\}$: \textbf{internal} (${\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\citep{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \textbf{deterministic} (${\tt det}$), a privileged-information strategy (an umbrella term: ground-truth simulator access in ScienceWorld, code interpreter in MATH, admissible-action enumeration in ALFWorld---see \S\ref{sec:cross-env} for environment-specific operationalizations) that eliminates observation uncertainty; these qualitative differences affect what routing signals the framework can detect. A \emph{routing policy} $\rho: \mathcal{O}^* \rightarrow \mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\rho$.
51	
52	\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}
53	\label{sec:collection}
54	
55	Per-step oracle labeling degenerates under sparse terminal rewards: on ScienceWorld \citep{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\% of steps (5{,}935 of 6{,}094 across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores, yielding no per-step routing signal. Episode-level comparison---directly measuring terminal outcomes---is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments.
56	
57	\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}
58	For a task instance $t$ and computation mode $m \in \mathcal{M}$, a \emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying mode $m$ at every step:
59	\begin{equation}
60	    \tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \ldots, o_T, a_T)
61	\end{equation}
62	where $T \leq T_{\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\tau_{\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\tau_{\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.
63	
64	Each trajectory $\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\tau) \in \mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\tau) \in \{0, 1\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:
65	\begin{multline}
66	    \bigl(s^{\tt int}, s^{\tt det}, d^{\tt int}, d^{\tt det}\bigr) \triangleq \\
67	    \bigl(s(\tau_{\tt int}),\, s(\tau_{\tt det}),\, d(\tau_{\tt int}),\, d(\tau_{\tt det})\bigr)
68	\end{multline}
69	
70	We use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM v0.21.0) on ScienceWorld \citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty; complete prompt templates are provided in Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-templates}. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \S\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\ref{sec:cross-env}.
71	
72	\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}
73	\label{sec:labeling}
74	
75	We define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:
76	\begin{multline}
77	    L\!\bigl(\tau_{\tt int}^{(t)}, \tau_{\tt det}^{(t)}\bigr) \;\rightarrow \\
78	    \ell \in \{{\tt det\text{-}pref},\; {\tt int\text{-}pref},\; {\tt no\text{-}pref}\}
79	\end{multline}
80	
81	\begin{table}[t]
82	\centering
83	\small
84	\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}
85	\toprule
86	Condition & Death\% & $N$ \\
87	\midrule
88	Single-trial baseline & 42.9 & 245 \\
89	Reflexion ($3{\times}$) & 31.4 & 220 \\
90	Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\times}$) & 30.6 & 255 \\
91	Deterministic baseline & 16.3 & 245 \\
92	\midrule
93	\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\textit{10 death-prone tasks only:}} \\
94	Death-prone baseline & 48.0 & 200 \\
95	Retry ($5{\times}$) & 43.5 & 200 \\
96	\bottomrule
97	\end{tabular}
98	\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under improvement methods. Top: all 11 tasks. Bottom: 10 death-prone tasks (\texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded). No intervention approaches deterministic-level safety.}
99	\label{tab:agent-improvement}
100	\end{table}
101	
102	The labeling function is \emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \texttt{no\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Label distributions are reported in \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}.
103	
104	\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}
105	\label{sec:daf}
106	
107	To quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\delta = \mathbb{E}[d^{\tt int}] - \mathbb{E}[d^{\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \bar{s}_{\text{surv}}$, where $\bar{s}_{\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\bar{s}_{\text{surv}} \ll P$ (in our data, $\bar{s}_{\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\approx}\,\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:
108	\begin{equation}
109	    \Delta_{\text{full}} \approx \underbrace{\delta \cdot P}_{\text{death contribution}} + \Delta_{\text{nd}}
110	\end{equation}
111	The approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\%$ of $\Delta_{\text{full}}$).
112	
113	We define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:
114	\begin{equation}
115	    \text{DAF} = 1 - \frac{\Delta_{\text{nd}}}{\Delta_{\text{full}}} \approx \frac{\delta P}{\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}}
116	\end{equation}%
117	\footnote{When $\Delta_{\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}
118	
119	DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Table~\ref{tab:penalty-sensitivity}); we treat the label fraction (78.9\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; death rates and $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\citep{ng1999policy}. Uncertainty is quantified via episode-level bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$, percentile 95\% CIs).
120	
121	\begin{table}[t]
122	\centering
123	\scriptsize
124	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
125	\begin{tabular}{@{}rrr@{}}
126	\toprule
127	$P$ & DAF($P$) & 95\% CI \\
128	\midrule
129	0 & 7.4\% & [$-39.4$, $82.5$] \\
130	1 & 14.8\% & [$-28.6$, $89.6$] \\
131	5 & 35.4\% & [$-1.8$, $92.7$] \\
132	10 & 50.4\% & [$18.5$, $93.3$] \\
133	25 & 70.7\% & [$48.0$, $96.3$] \\
134	50 & 82.6\% & [$67.2$, $98.2$] \\
135	100\textsuperscript{*} & 90.4\% & [$81.3$, $99.1$] \\
136	200 & 94.9\% & [$89.9$, $99.5$] \\
137	500 & 97.9\% & [$95.7$, $99.8$] \\
138	\bottomrule
139	\end{tabular}
140	\caption{Sensitivity of DAF to death penalty magnitude $P$. $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$ is invariant; as $P$ increases, $\Delta_{\text{full}} \approx \delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}$ grows, mechanically increasing DAF. \textsuperscript{*}Default ScienceWorld setting. Bootstrap 95\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$).}
141	\label{tab:penalty-sensitivity}
142	\end{table}
143	
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Large language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.
4	In ScienceWorld \citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone, as the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated), while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}
5	
6	Existing routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \citep{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \citep{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \citep{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026}, model tiers \citep{mtrouter-2026}, or episode-level model selection under sparse rewards \citep{budget-aware-routing-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. These approaches, which operate in non-catastrophic settings, share the assumption that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---an assumption that remains untested: no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing preferences in interactive environments.
7	
8	We propose a \emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding. Always-deterministic matches 96.3\% of oracle decisions, capping routing's marginal value at 2.89 points (\S\ref{sec:baselines}); removing death episodes reveals that oracle access provides almost no non-death advantage ($\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$, 9.6\% of total gap; \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}). Two independent 70B-class models yield DAF${}=92.4$--$101.0\%$ (Llama-70B exceeds 100\%: death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap), and Reflexion ($N{=}3$) similarly drives DAF above 100\% (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).
9	
10	This addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\% tie rate; \S\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\S\ref{sec:baselines}). Our approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \citep{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, comparing episode outcomes.
11	
12	Cross-context checks probe specificity: MATH Level~4--5 ($N{=}795$, code interpreter) shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$, $p = 0.81$); Llama-3.1-8B replication ($N{=}220$) confirms death dominance across model families (direction consistency 10/10; \S\ref{sec:cross-model}). Four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) reduce death rates but none closes the gap to deterministic safety, with ${\geq}$12~pp residual gaps (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).
13	An exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
14	
15	Our contributions: (1)~an \textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\% tie rate), decomposes routing signal via penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics, and provides build-vs-buy signal from a ${\sim}$50-episode pilot (Appendix~\ref{app:decision-framework}); (2)~\textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels} in ScienceWorld stem from death asymmetry---replicated across three 7--9B model families (DAF $88$--$90\%$), four interventions, $10{\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models (DAF $92.4$--$101.0\%$), and cross-environment probes (MATH, ALFWorld)---while a step-level router recovers only task-type classification (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\ref{sec:cross-model}).
16	
17	
18

      "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nIn ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone, as the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated), while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \\citep{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \\citep{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\citep{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026}, model tiers \\citep{mtrouter-2026}, or episode-level model selection under sparse rewards \\citep{budget-aware-routing-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. These approaches, which operate in non-catastrophic settings, share the assumption that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---an assumption that remains untested: no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing preferences in interactive environments.\n\nWe propose a \\emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding. Always-deterministic matches 96.3\\% of oracle decisions, capping routing's marginal value at 2.89 points (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}); removing death episodes reveals that oracle access provides almost no non-death advantage ($\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$, 9.6\\% of total gap; \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}). Two independent 70B-class models yield DAF${}=92.4$--$101.0\\%$ (Llama-70B exceeds 100\\%: death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap), and Reflexion ($N{=}3$) similarly drives DAF above 100\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\n\nThis addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\\% tie rate; \\S\\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}). Our approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\citep{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, comparing episode outcomes.\n\nCross-context checks probe specificity: MATH Level~4--5 ($N{=}795$, code interpreter) shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$); Llama-3.1-8B replication ($N{=}220$) confirms death dominance across model families (direction consistency 10/10; \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}). Four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) reduce death rates but none closes the gap to deterministic safety, with ${\\geq}$12~pp residual gaps (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\nAn exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions: (1)~an \\textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\\% tie rate), decomposes routing signal via penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics, and provides build-vs-buy signal from a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot (Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}); (2)~\\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels} in ScienceWorld stem from death asymmetry---replicated across three 7--9B model families (DAF $88$--$90\\%$), four interventions, $10{\\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models (DAF $92.4$--$101.0\\%$), and cross-environment probes (MATH, ALFWorld)---while a step-level router recovers only task-type classification (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\n\n",

    "newString": "Our contributions: (1)~an \\textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\\% tie rate), decomposes routing signal via penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics, and provides build-vs-buy signal from a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot (Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}); (2)~\\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels} in ScienceWorld stem from death asymmetry---replicated across three 7--9B model families (DAF $88$--$90\\%$), four interventions, $10{\\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models (DAF $92.4$--$101.0\\%$), and cross-environment probes (MATH, ALFWorld)---while a step-level router recovers only task-type classification (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure*}\n",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nIn ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone, as the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated), while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \\citep{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \\citep{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\citep{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026}, model tiers \\citep{mtrouter-2026}, or episode-level model selection under sparse rewards \\citep{budget-aware-routing-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. These approaches, which operate in non-catastrophic settings, share the assumption that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---an assumption that remains untested: no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing preferences in interactive environments.\n\nWe propose a \\emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding. Always-deterministic matches 96.3\\% of oracle decisions, capping routing's marginal value at 2.89 points (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}); removing death episodes reveals that oracle access provides almost no non-death advantage ($\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$, 9.6\\% of total gap; \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}). Two independent 70B-class models yield DAF${}=92.4$--$101.0\\%$ (Llama-70B exceeds 100\\%: death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap), and Reflexion ($N{=}3$) similarly drives DAF above 100\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\n\nThis addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\\% tie rate; \\S\\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}). Our approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\citep{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, comparing episode outcomes.\n\nCross-context checks probe specificity: MATH Level~4--5 ($N{=}795$, code interpreter) shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$); Llama-3.1-8B replication ($N{=}220$) confirms death dominance across model families (direction consistency 10/10; \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}). Four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) reduce death rates but none closes the gap to deterministic safety, with ${\\geq}$12~pp residual gaps (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\nAn exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions: (1)~an \\textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\\% tie rate), decomposes routing signal via penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics, and provides build-vs-buy signal from a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot (Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}); (2)~\\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels} in ScienceWorld stem from death asymmetry---replicated across three 7--9B model families (DAF $88$--$90\\%$), four interventions, $10{\\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models (DAF $92.4$--$101.0\\%$), and cross-environment probes (MATH, ALFWorld)---while a step-level router recovers only task-type classification (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\n\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 14,
        "oldLines": 4,
        "newStart": 14,
        "newLines": 9,
        "lines": [
          " ",
          " Our contributions: (1)~an \\textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\\% tie rate), decomposes routing signal via penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics, and provides build-vs-buy signal from a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot (Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}); (2)~\\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels} in ScienceWorld stem from death asymmetry---replicated across three 7--9B model families (DAF $88$--$90\\%$), four interventions, $10{\\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models (DAF $92.4$--$101.0\\%$), and cross-environment probes (MATH, ALFWorld)---while a step-level router recovers only task-type classification (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:cross-model}).",
          " ",

  "old_string": "Our diagnostic framework (Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}) quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}\n\\label{alg:labeling}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$\n\\ENSURE Label $\\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}$\n\\FOR{each $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$}\n    \\STATE Run episode $\\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$\n    \\STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\IF{$d_{\\tt int} = 1$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 0$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = 0$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{both dead}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt det} > s_{\\tt int}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt int} > s_{\\tt det}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSE\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{tied alive}\n\\ENDIF\n\\RETURN $\\ell$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}",
  "new_string": "Our diagnostic framework (Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}) quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}).     "newString": "Our diagnostic framework (Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}) quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/label_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Episode-level label decomposition ($N{=}245$). Of 95 non-tie labels, 75 (78.9\\%) arise from death asymmetry: 54 where death is the sole discriminator and 21 with co-occurring score differences. All 20 non-death labels originate from a single task type (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing}).}\n\\label{fig:label-decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur diagnostic framework (Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}) quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}\n\\label{alg:labeling}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$\n\\ENSURE Label $\\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}$\n\\FOR{each $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$}\n    \\STATE Run episode $\\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$\n    \\STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\IF{$d_{\\tt int} = 1$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 0$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = 0$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{both dead}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt det} > s_{\\tt int}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt int} > s_{\\tt det}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSE\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{tied alive}\n\\ENDIF\n\\RETURN $\\ell$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment with observation space $\\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic} (${\\tt det}$), a privileged-information strategy (an umbrella term: ground-truth simulator access in ScienceWorld, code interpreter in MATH, admissible-action enumeration in ALFWorld---see \\S\\ref{sec:cross-env} for environment-specific operationalizations) that eliminates observation uncertainty; these qualitative differences affect what routing signals the framework can detect. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$.\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling degenerates under sparse terminal rewards: on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps (5{,}935 of 6{,}094 across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores, yielding no per-step routing signal. Episode-level comparison---directly measuring terminal outcomes---is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and computation mode $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying mode $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM v0.21.0) on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty; complete prompt templates are provided in Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-templates}. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Death\\% & $N$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-trial baseline & 42.9 & 245 \\\\\nReflexion ($3{\\times}$) & 31.4 & 220 \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$) & 30.6 & 255 \\\\\nDeterministic baseline & 16.3 & 245 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\\textit{10 death-prone tasks only:}} \\\\\nDeath-prone baseline & 48.0 & 200 \\\\\nRetry ($5{\\times}$) & 43.5 & 200 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under improvement methods. Top: all 11 tasks. Bottom: 10 death-prone tasks (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded). No intervention approaches deterministic-level safety.}\n\\label{tab:agent-improvement}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \\texttt{no\\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nDAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Table~\\ref{tab:penalty-sensitivity}); we treat the label fraction (78.9\\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\\citep{ng1999policy}. Uncertainty is quantified via episode-level bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$, percentile 95\\% CIs).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\scriptsize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rrr@{}}\n\\toprule\n$P$ & DAF($P$) & 95\\% CI \\\\\n\\midrule\n0 & 7.4\\% & [$-39.4$, $82.5$] \\\\\n1 & 14.8\\% & [$-28.6$, $89.6$] \\\\\n5 & 35.4\\% & [$-1.8$, $92.7$] \\\\\n10 & 50.4\\% & [$18.5$, $93.3$] \\\\\n25 & 70.7\\% & [$48.0$, $96.3$] \\\\\n50 & 82.6\\% & [$67.2$, $98.2$] \\\\\n100\\textsuperscript{*} & 90.4\\% & [$81.3$, $99.1$] \\\\\n200 & 94.9\\% & [$89.9$, $99.5$] \\\\\n500 & 97.9\\% & [$95.7$, $99.8$] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Sensitivity of DAF to death penalty magnitude $P$. $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = 2.84$ is invariant; as $P$ increases, $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ grows, mechanically increasing DAF. \\textsuperscript{*}Default ScienceWorld setting. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$).}\n\\label{tab:penalty-sensitivity}\n\\end{table}\n",
