    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/label_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Episode-level label decomposition ($N{=}245$). Of 95 non-tie labels, 75 (78.9\\%) arise from death asymmetry: 54 where death is the sole discriminator and 21 with co-occurring score differences. All 20 non-death labels originate from a single task type (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing}).}\n\\label{fig:label-decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur diagnostic framework (Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}) quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment with observation space $\\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic} (${\\tt det}$), a privileged-information strategy (an umbrella term: ground-truth simulator access in ScienceWorld, code interpreter in MATH, admissible-action enumeration in ALFWorld---see \\S\\ref{sec:cross-env} for environment-specific operationalizations) that eliminates observation uncertainty; these qualitative differences affect what routing signals the framework can detect. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$.\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling degenerates under sparse terminal rewards: on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps (5{,}935 of 6{,}094 across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores, yielding no per-step routing signal. Episode-level comparison---directly measuring terminal outcomes---is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and computation mode $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying mode $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM v0.21.0) on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty; complete prompt templates are provided in Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-templates}. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Death\\% & $N$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-trial baseline & 42.9 & 245 \\\\\nReflexion ($3{\\times}$) & 31.4 & 220 \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$) & 30.6 & 255 \\\\\nDeterministic baseline & 16.3 & 245 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\\textit{10 death-prone tasks only:}} \\\\\nDeath-prone baseline & 48.0 & 200 \\\\\nRetry ($5{\\times}$) & 43.5 & 200 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under improvement methods. Top: all 11 tasks. Bottom: 10 death-prone tasks (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded). No intervention approaches deterministic-level safety.}\n\\label{tab:agent-improvement}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \\texttt{no\\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nDAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Table~\\ref{tab:penalty-sensitivity}); we treat the label fraction (78.9\\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\\citep{ng1999policy}. Uncertainty is quantified via episode-level bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$, percentile 95\\% CIs).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\scriptsize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rrr@{}}\n\\toprule\n$P$ & DAF($P$) & 95\\% CI \\\\\n\\midrule\n0 & 7.4\\% & [$-39.4$, $82.5$] \\\\\n1 & 14.8\\% & [$-28.6$, $89.6$] \\\\\n5 & 35.4\\% & [$-1.8$, $92.7$] \\\\\n10 & 50.4\\% & [$18.5$, $93.3$] \\\\\n25 & 70.7\\% & [$48.0$, $96.3$] \\\\\n50 & 82.6\\% & [$67.2$, $98.2$] \\\\\n100\\textsuperscript{*} & 90.4\\% & [$81.3$, $99.1$] \\\\\n200 & 94.9\\% & [$89.9$, $99.5$] \\\\\n500 & 97.9\\% & [$95.7$, $99.8$] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Sensitivity of DAF to death penalty magnitude $P$. $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = 2.84$ is invariant; as $P$ increases, $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ grows, mechanically increasing DAF. \\textsuperscript{*}Default ScienceWorld setting. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$).}\n\\label{tab:penalty-sensitivity}\n\\end{table}\n",

120	\label{sec:agent-improvement}
121	
122	We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$) yields marginal reduction ($43.5\%$ vs.\ $48.0\%$). Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death to $31.4\%$ but the gap to deterministic remains $15.1$~pp; under matched-episode analysis ($N{=}3$), $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF${}>100\%$. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($N{=}255$) reduces death rate to $30.6\%$ with DAF $= 92.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[86.1\%, 97.9\%]$). A second 70B model, Llama-3.1-70B ($N{=}220$), yields DAF $= 101.0\%$ ($[95.3\%, 107.5\%]$): death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap. Two 70B-class models confirm that scaling intensifies rather than attenuates death dominance.
123	
124	\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}
125	We extend both interventions to $N{=}5$ trials on death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$; Figure~\ref{fig:dose-response}). Retry is nearly flat ($48.0\% \to 43.5\%$). Reflexion drops steeply to $34.0\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $p = 0.003$) then stabilizes ($34$--$36.5\%$ for $N \geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$); both the decline and plateau are well-powered (MDE ${\sim}8$~pp at 80\% power; Appendix~\ref{app:dose-response}).
126	
127	\begin{figure}[t]
128	\centering
129	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/dose_response.pdf}
130	\caption{Death rate vs.\ trial count for Retry and Reflexion on 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$). Reflexion drops steeply through $N{=}3$ then plateaus; Retry is nearly flat. Horizontal lines: 72B ($30.6\%$) and deterministic ($16.3\%$) baselines. Error bands: Wilson 95\% CIs.}
131	\label{fig:dose-response}
132	\end{figure}
133	
134	\subsection{Cross-Environment Boundary Probes}
135	\label{sec:math-contrast}
136	\label{sec:cross-env}
137	
138	We probe two environments as boundary cases to test the framework's specificity (Figure~\ref{fig:signal-reversal}). On MATH Level~4--5~\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$), internal reasoning and code-interpreter accuracy are near-identical ($73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$, $p = 0.81$): without a catastrophic failure mechanism, no routing signal emerges despite substantial task difficulty. ALFWorld~\citep{alfworld-2021} (135 games, no death mechanism, DAF $= 0\%$) shows 94.6\% of non-tie labels favoring the information-advantaged strategy (which enumerates admissible actions); Llama-3.1-8B replicates at 100\%. The framework diagnoses this qualitatively different mechanism---information asymmetry rather than death avoidance---without modification, confirming that DAF correctly attributes zero signal to death when none exists.

    "file": {
      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
      "content": "\\label{sec:agent-improvement}\n\nWe test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$) yields marginal reduction ($43.5\\%$ vs.\\ $48.0\\%$). Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death to $31.4\\%$ but the gap to deterministic remains $15.1$~pp; under matched-episode analysis ($N{=}3$), $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF${}>100\\%$. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($N{=}255$) reduces death rate to $30.6\\%$ with DAF $= 92.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[86.1\\%, 97.9\\%]$). A second 70B model, Llama-3.1-70B ($N{=}220$), yields DAF $= 101.0\\%$ ($[95.3\\%, 107.5\\%]$): death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap. Two 70B-class models confirm that scaling intensifies rather than attenuates death dominance.\n\n\\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}\nWe extend both interventions to $N{=}5$ trials on death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$; Figure~\\ref{fig:dose-response}). Retry is nearly flat ($48.0\\% \\to 43.5\\%$). Reflexion drops steeply to $34.0\\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $p = 0.003$) then stabilizes ($34$--$36.5\\%$ for $N \\geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$); both the decline and plateau are well-powered (MDE ${\\sim}8$~pp at 80\\% power; Appendix~\\ref{app:dose-response}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Death rate vs.\\ trial count for Retry and Reflexion on 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$). Reflexion drops steeply through $N{=}3$ then plateaus; Retry is nearly flat. Horizontal lines: 72B ($30.6\\%$) and deterministic ($16.3\\%$) baselines. Error bands: Wilson 95\\% CIs.}\n\\label{fig:dose-response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Environment Boundary Probes}\n\\label{sec:math-contrast}\n\\label{sec:cross-env}\n\nWe probe two environments as boundary cases to test the framework's specificity (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal-reversal}). On MATH Level~4--5~\\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$), internal reasoning and code-interpreter accuracy are near-identical ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$): without a catastrophic failure mechanism, no routing signal emerges despite substantial task difficulty. ALFWorld~\\citep{alfworld-2021} (135 games, no death mechanism, DAF $= 0\\%$) shows 94.6\\% of non-tie labels favoring the information-advantaged strategy (which enumerates admissible actions); Llama-3.1-8B replicates at 100\\%. The framework diagnoses this qualitatively different mechanism---information asymmetry rather than death avoidance---without modification, confirming that DAF correctly attributes zero signal to death when none exists.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.95\\columnwidth]{figures/signal_reversal.pdf}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\caption{Cross-environment label distributions. ScienceWorld and ALFWorld both favor deterministic via different mechanisms (death avoidance vs.\\ information asymmetry); MATH shows no routing signal.}\n\\label{fig:signal-reversal}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Baselines}\n\\label{sec:baselines}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & Score & 95\\% CI & Death \\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nAlways-Internal & $-39.07$ & [$-46.40$, $-32.27$] & 42.9 \\\\\nAlways-Deterministic & $-9.47$ & [$-14.71$, $-4.29$] & 16.3 \\\\\nRandom & $-22.38$ & [$-28.67$, $-15.75$] & 27.8 \\\\\nOracle & $-6.58$ & [$-11.54$, $-1.70$] & 14.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTrained Router$^\\dagger$ & $-10.70$ & [$-16.33$, $-5.27$] & 17.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Episode-weighted mean scores on ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes). $\\dagger$\\,Single seed (42); see Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness} for 8-seed robustness.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}",
      "numLines": 50,
      "startLine": 120,
      "totalLines": 177
    }
  },

```text
168	\label{tab:baselines}
169	\end{table}
170	
171	Always-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}). Random routing ($-22.38$) falls between the two pure strategies, confirming that the gap is not an artifact of evaluation methodology. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\% of decisions matching always-deterministic---an \emph{oracle degeneracy} where perfect per-episode foresight provides marginal benefit over the trivial always-deterministic baseline (Appendix~\ref{sec:threshold-routing}).
172	
173	\subsection{Router Training: Inconclusive Results}
174	\label{sec:router-diagnostic}
175	
176	A Qwen3 LoRA router (0.6B--4B; Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}) trained on counterfactual labels shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$) across all model scales; episode-level evaluation yields $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$, below majority-vote.\footnote{Consistent with noisy-label bounds~\citep{natarajan2013learning}: ${\sim}$88\% of step-level labels are uninformative with 90.5\%/3.7\% class imbalance (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}).} Leave-one-task-out degrades to chance ($49.2\%$), confirming signal is a per-task-type constant rather than a learnable function of step-level observations. Deployed end-to-end, the best router (4B) scores $-10.70$, not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$); this negative result is consistent with the death-dominance finding, as death avoidance cannot be predicted from pre-episode features when it depends on multi-step trajectory dynamics.
177

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "98064521-21a7-47f5-80fb-8a5838c73e38",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/method.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/label_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Episode-level label decomposition ($N{=}245$). Of 95 non-tie labels, 75 (78.9\\%) arise from death asymmetry: 54 where death is the sole discriminator and 21 with co-occurring score differences. All 20 non-death labels originate from a single task type (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing}).}\n\\label{fig:label-decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur diagnostic framework (Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}) quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment with observation space $\\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic} (${\\tt det}$), a privileged-information strategy (an umbrella term: ground-truth simulator access in ScienceWorld, code interpreter in MATH, admissible-action enumeration in ALFWorld---see \\S\\ref{sec:cross-env} for environment-specific operationalizations) that eliminates observation uncertainty; these qualitative differences affect what routing signals the framework can detect. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$.\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling degenerates under sparse terminal rewards: on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps (5{,}935 of 6{,}094 across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores, yielding no per-step routing signal. Episode-level comparison---directly measuring terminal outcomes---is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and computation mode $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying mode $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM v0.21.0) on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty; complete prompt templates are provided in Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-templates}. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}\n\\label{alg:labeling}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$\n\\ENSURE Label $\\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}$\n\\FOR{each $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$}\n    \\STATE Run episode $\\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$\n    \\STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\IF{$d_{\\tt int} = 1$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 0$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = 0$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{both dead}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt det} > s_{\\tt int}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt int} > s_{\\tt det}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSE\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{tied alive}\n\\ENDIF\n\\RETURN $\\ell$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Death\\% & $N$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-trial baseline & 42.9 & 245 \\\\\nReflexion ($3{\\times}$) & 31.4 & 220 \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$) & 30.6 & 255 \\\\\nDeterministic baseline & 16.3 & 245 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\\textit{10 death-prone tasks only:}} \\\\\nDeath-prone baseline & 48.0 & 200 \\\\\nRetry ($5{\\times}$) & 43.5 & 200 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under improvement methods. Top: all 11 tasks. Bottom: 10 death-prone tasks (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded). No intervention approaches deterministic-level safety.}\n\\label{tab:agent-improvement}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \\texttt{no\\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nDAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Table~\\ref{tab:penalty-sensitivity}); we treat the label fraction (78.9\\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\\citep{ng1999policy}. Uncertainty is quantified via episode-level bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$, percentile 95\\% CIs).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\scriptsize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rrr@{}}\n\\toprule\n$P$ & DAF($P$) & 95\\% CI \\\\\n\\midrule\n0 & 7.4\\% & [$-39.4$, $82.5$] \\\\\n1 & 14.8\\% & [$-28.6$, $89.6$] \\\\\n5 & 35.4\\% & [$-1.8$, $92.7$] \\\\\n10 & 50.4\\% & [$18.5$, $93.3$] \\\\\n25 & 70.7\\% & [$48.0$, $96.3$] \\\\\n50 & 82.6\\% & [$67.2$, $98.2$] \\\\\n100\\textsuperscript{*} & 90.4\\% & [$81.3$, $99.1$] \\\\\n200 & 94.9\\% & [$89.9$, $99.5$] \\\\\n500 & 97.9\\% & [$95.7$, $99.8$] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Sensitivity of DAF to death penalty magnitude $P$. $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = 2.84$ is invariant; as $P$ increases, $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ grows, mechanically increasing DAF. \\textsuperscript{*}Default ScienceWorld setting. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$).}\n\\label{tab:penalty-sensitivity}\n\\end{table}\n",
        "numLines": 136,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 136
      }
    },

  },

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nIn ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone, as the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated), while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \\citep{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \\citep{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\citep{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026}, model tiers \\citep{mtrouter-2026}, or episode-level model selection under sparse rewards \\citep{budget-aware-routing-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. These approaches, which operate in non-catastrophic settings, share the assumption that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---an assumption that remains untested: no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing preferences in interactive environments.\n\nWe propose a \\emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding. Always-deterministic matches 96.3\\% of oracle decisions, capping routing's marginal value at 2.89 points (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}); removing death episodes reveals that oracle access provides almost no non-death advantage ($\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$, 9.6\\% of total gap; \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}). Two independent 70B-class models yield DAF${}=92.4$--$101.0\\%$ (Llama-70B exceeds 100\\%: death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap), and Reflexion ($N{=}3$) similarly drives DAF above 100\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\n\nThis addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\\% tie rate; \\S\\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}). Our approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\citep{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, comparing episode outcomes.\n\nCross-context checks probe specificity: MATH Level~4--5 ($N{=}795$, code interpreter) shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$); Llama-3.1-8B replication ($N{=}220$) confirms death dominance across model families (direction consistency 10/10; \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}). Four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) reduce death rates but none closes the gap to deterministic safety, with ${\\geq}$12~pp residual gaps (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\nAn exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions: (1)~an \\textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\\% tie rate), decomposes routing signal via penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics, and provides build-vs-buy signal from a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot (Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}); (2)~\\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels} in ScienceWorld stem from death asymmetry---replicated across three 7--9B model families (DAF $88$--$90\\%$), four interventions, $10{\\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models (DAF $92.4$--$101.0\\%$), and cross-environment probes (MATH, ALFWorld)---while a step-level router recovers only task-type classification (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure*}\n",
        "numLines": 23,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 23
      }
    },

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Signal granularity and router failure.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property, but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories rather than individual observations. Six router variants (0.6B--4B, with and without augmentation) converge on near-chance balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$), and leave-one-task-out evaluation degrades to $49.2\\%$, confirming that the router captures only task-type identity rather than generalizable step-level routing signal.\n\nTwo a priori unpredictable results emerge: $10{\\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models fails to eliminate death dominance (DAF${}=92.4$--$101.0\\%$; Llama-70B exceeds 100\\%), and Reflexion yields DAF${}>100\\%$. The cross-model death-rate stability ($36$--$49\\%$ across five models) confirms death vulnerability is a task-environment property, not a model-specific artifact. This suggests that routing in death-dominated environments is better addressed through action-space constraints (Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}) than through learned step-level policies.\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy and practical implications.}\nAlways-deterministic matches 96.3\\% of oracle decisions (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}), capping routing's marginal value at 2.89 points---an \\emph{oracle degeneracy} where perfect per-episode foresight provides negligible benefit over a trivial baseline. This degeneracy arises because $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = 2.84$ (9.6\\% of total gap): the advantage concentrates almost entirely in death episodes, and the oracle's only meaningful contribution is avoiding the few cases where deterministic performs worse on non-death episodes.\n\nFor practitioners, this suggests a concrete decision procedure: if a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot reveals DAF $> 80\\%$, always-deterministic is likely near-optimal and building a learned router offers minimal return (Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}). ALFWorld (DAF${=}0\\%$) confirms the framework diagnoses qualitatively different mechanisms---there, routing signal stems from information asymmetry rather than catastrophic failure, indicating that a learned router may indeed add value.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and applicability conditions.}\nThe decomposition requires three conditions: (i)~binary catastrophic failures with fixed penalty, (ii)~asymmetric failure rates between strategies, and (iii)~$P \\gg \\Delta_\\text{nd}$ so that death dominates the score gap. ScienceWorld satisfies all three; MATH and ALFWorld lack the first. Environments with graded penalties, stochastic failure severity, or symmetric failure rates would require extensions to the DAF framework. Action-space filtering reduces death rate by 19~pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}), suggesting that targeted safety interventions may be more effective than general-purpose routing in death-dominated settings.\n\n",
        "numLines": 17,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 17
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/discussion.tex"
  },

1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3

7	\caption{Per-task death rate differential vs.\ performance gap ($n{=}11$ task types). The near-perfect correlation ($r = 0.97$) is a structural consequence of death dominance (DAF $\to 1 \Rightarrow r \to 1$), not an independent finding. The sole outlier (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, red diamond) has zero deaths; its $\Delta_{\text{full}} = +9.58$ arises entirely from non-death score differences.}
8	\label{fig:decomposition}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	We analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types lack actionable routing signal due to development contamination (5), taxonomic redundancy (7), or agent-floor performance (7). An anti-cherry-picking analysis confirms death dominance holds in 18 of these 19 types ($\overline{\Delta\text{DR}} = +38.2$\,pp $>$ included tasks' $+28.7$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:per-task}).
12	
13	\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}
14	\label{sec:death-decomp}
15	
16	\begin{table}[t]

40	\caption{Per-task decomposition. D\%: death rate for internal/deterministic. D/I/N: det-preferred / int-preferred / no-preference labels. $\dagger$\,Only task with zero deaths; $n{=}40$ (two batches).}
41	\label{tab:per-task}

43	
44	Of the 95 non-tie routing labels (Figure~\ref{fig:label-decomposition}), 75 (78.9\%; 95\% CI: $[70.7\%, 87.0\%]$, episode-level bootstrap) arise from death asymmetry: the internal strategy triggers a catastrophic $-100$ penalty while the deterministic strategy survives. This penalty-independent label fraction is the primary diagnostic metric. Of these, 54 are sole-signal labels where death was the only discriminator; at $P{=}0$ these reduce to ties, but the labeling function itself is penalty-independent (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}).
45	
46	DAF $= 90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$ ($\Delta_{\text{full}} = +29.60$, $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$; 95\% CI$_{\text{DAF}}$: $[81.3\%, 99.1\%]$, episode-level bootstrap). The 78.9\% label fraction provides the more robust, penalty-invariant characterization (\S\ref{sec:daf}).
47	
48	\paragraph{Per-task structure.}
49	The internal strategy dies in 42.9\% of episodes versus 16.3\% for deterministic; all 105 internal deaths are triggered by the \texttt{focus on} action (Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}). Per-task $\Delta$DR correlates with $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ at $r = 0.97$ (Figure~\ref{fig:decomposition}), a structural consequence of DAF$\to 1$ rather than an independent finding. Figure~\ref{fig:death-timeline} illustrates the typical death trajectory.
50	
51	\begin{figure}[t]
52	\centering
53	\includegraphics[width=0.95\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_timeline.pdf}
54	\vspace{-2mm}
55	\caption{Action sequences for 6 representative death episodes. The fatal \texttt{focus on} (arrow) appears in final steps after long \texttt{connect} sequences (``connect loop $\to$ fatal focus'').}
56	\label{fig:death-timeline}
57	\end{figure}
58	
59	\paragraph{Binary structure and robustness.}
60	All 10 death-prone task types produce 100\% death-asymmetry labels; only \texttt{find-non-living-thing} (zero deaths) produces non-death signal (SCI $= 0.21$; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}). Leave-one-task-out analysis (Table~\ref{tab:loto-daf}) confirms robustness: removing any single death-prone task changes DAF by at most 3.0~pp. Bootstrap 95\% CIs: DAF $[81.3\%, 99.1\%]$; cluster-robust $[73.3\%, 100.0\%]$ ($K{=}11$; Appendix~\ref{app:wild-cluster}). Two independent replications (Gemma-2-9B-it, DAF${=}89.7\%$; seed=123, DAF${=}89.7\%$) converge within 0.7~pp.

114	\label{tab:cross-model-env}
115	\end{table}
116	
117	Llama-3.1-8B~\citep{llama3-2024} ($N{=}220$) and Gemma-2-9B-it~\citep{gemma2-2024} ($N{=}220$) independently replicate death dominance: DAF $= 88.2\%$ and $89.7\%$ respectively, with direction consistency 10/10 on death-prone tasks (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}). Three architecturally distinct model families---Qwen (Alibaba), Llama (Meta), Gemma (Google)---converge on DAF $88$--$90\%$ with a spread of only 2.2~pp, despite differences in pretraining data, architecture, and tokenization. This narrow convergence suggests that death dominance is an environment-level property rather than a model-specific artifact. Scaling $10{\times}$ to two 70B-class models (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) intensifies rather than attenuates the pattern (DAF${=}92.4$--$101.0\%$), contrary to the intuition that larger models would reduce the failure gap.
118	
119	\subsection{Agent Improvement Baselines}
120	\label{sec:agent-improvement}
121	
122	We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$) yields marginal reduction ($43.5\%$ vs.\ $48.0\%$). Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death to $31.4\%$ but the gap to deterministic remains $15.1$~pp; under matched-episode analysis ($N{=}3$), $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF${}>100\%$. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($N{=}255$) reduces death rate to $30.6\%$ with DAF $= 92.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[86.1\%, 97.9\%]$). A second 70B model, Llama-3.1-70B ($N{=}220$), yields DAF $= 101.0\%$ ($[95.3\%, 107.5\%]$): death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap. Two 70B-class models confirm that scaling intensifies rather than attenuates death dominance.
123	
124	\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}
125	We extend both interventions to $N{=}5$ trials on death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$; Figure~\ref{fig:dose-response}). Retry is nearly flat ($48.0\% \to 43.5\%$). Reflexion drops steeply to $34.0\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $p = 0.003$) then stabilizes ($34$--$36.5\%$ for $N \geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$); both the decline and plateau are well-powered (MDE ${\sim}8$~pp at 80\% power; Appendix~\ref{app:dose-response}).
126	
127	\begin{figure}[t]
128	\centering
129	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/dose_response.pdf}
130	\caption{Death rate vs.\ trial count for Retry and Reflexion on 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$). Reflexion drops steeply through $N{=}3$ then plateaus; Retry is nearly flat. Horizontal lines: 72B ($30.6\%$) and deterministic ($16.3\%$) baselines. Error bands: Wilson 95\% CIs.}
131	\label{fig:dose-response}
132	\end{figure}
133	
134	\subsection{Cross-Environment Boundary Probes}
135	\label{sec:math-contrast}
136	\label{sec:cross-env}
137	
138	We probe two environments as boundary cases to test the framework's specificity (Figure~\ref{fig:signal-reversal}). On MATH Level~4--5~\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$), internal reasoning and code-interpreter accuracy are near-identical ($73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$, $p = 0.81$): without a catastrophic failure mechanism, no routing signal emerges despite substantial task difficulty. ALFWorld~\citep{alfworld-2021} (135 games, no death mechanism, DAF $= 0\%$) shows 94.6\% of non-tie labels favoring the information-advantaged strategy (which enumerates admissible actions); Llama-3.1-8B replicates at 100\%. The framework diagnoses this qualitatively different mechanism---information asymmetry rather than death avoidance---without modification, confirming that DAF correctly attributes zero signal to death when none exists.
139	
140	\begin{figure}[t]
141	\centering
142	\includegraphics[width=0.95\columnwidth]{figures/signal_reversal.pdf}
143	\vspace{-2mm}
144	\caption{Cross-environment label distributions. ScienceWorld and ALFWorld both favor deterministic via different mechanisms (death avoidance vs.\ information asymmetry); MATH shows no routing signal.}
145	\label{fig:signal-reversal}
146	\end{figure}
147	
148	\subsection{Baselines}
149	\label{sec:baselines}
150	
151	Always-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}). Random routing ($-22.38$) falls between the two pure strategies, confirming that the gap is not an artifact of evaluation methodology. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\% of decisions matching always-deterministic---an \emph{oracle degeneracy} where perfect per-episode foresight provides marginal benefit over the trivial always-deterministic baseline (Appendix~\ref{sec:threshold-routing}).
152	
153	\subsection{Router Training: Inconclusive Results}
154	\label{sec:router-diagnostic}
155	
156	A Qwen3 LoRA router (0.6B--4B; Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}) trained on counterfactual labels shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$) across all model scales; episode-level evaluation yields $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$, below majority-vote.\footnote{Consistent with noisy-label bounds~\citep{natarajan2013learning}: ${\sim}$88\% of step-level labels are uninformative with 90.5\%/3.7\% class imbalance (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}).} Leave-one-task-out degrades to chance ($49.2\%$), confirming signal is a per-task-type constant rather than a learnable function of step-level observations. Deployed end-to-end, the best router (4B) scores $-10.70$, not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$); this negative result is consistent with the death-dominance finding, as death avoidance cannot be predicted from pre-episode features when it depends on multi-step trajectory dynamics.
157

SW = ScienceWorld, ALF = ALFWorld. Death dominance replicates across three 7--9B families and intensifies under $10{\\times}$ scaling; ALFWorld shows information-asymmetry signal; MATH shows none. DR = death rate (\\%).}\n\\label{tab:cross-model-env}\n\\end{table}\n\nLlama-3.1-8B~\\citep{llama3-2024} ($N{=}220$) and Gemma-2-9B-it~\\citep{gemma2-2024} ($N{=}220$) independently replicate death dominance: DAF $= 88.2\\%$ and $89.7\\%$ respectively, with direction consistency 10/10 on death-prone tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}). Three architecturally distinct model families---Qwen (Alibaba), Llama (Meta), Gemma (Google)---converge on DAF $88$--$90\\%$ with a spread of only 2.2~pp, despite differences in pretraining data, architecture, and tokenization. This narrow convergence suggests that death dominance is an environment-level property rather than a model-specific artifact. Scaling $10{\\times}$ to two 70B-class models (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) intensifies rather than attenuates the pattern (DAF${=}92.4$--$101.0\\%$), contrary to the intuition that larger models would reduce the failure gap.\n\n\\subsection{Agent Improvement Baselines}\n\\label{sec:agent-improvement}\n\nWe test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$) yields marginal reduction ($43.5\\%$ vs.\\ $48.0\\%$). Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death to $31.4\\%$ but the gap to deterministic remains $15.1$~pp; under matched-episode analysis ($N{=}3$), $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF${}>100\\%$. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($N{=}255$) reduces death rate to $30.6\\%$ with DAF $= 92.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[86.1\\%, 97.9\\%]$). A second 70B model, Llama-3.1-70B ($N{=}220$), yields DAF $= 101.0\\%$ ($[95.3\\%, 107.5\\%]$): death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap. Two 70B-class models confirm that scaling intensifies rather than attenuates death dominance.\n\n\\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}\nWe extend both interventions to $N{=}5$ trials on death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$; Figure~\\ref{fig:dose-response}). Retry is nearly flat ($48.0\\% \\to 43.5\\%$). Reflexion drops steeply to $34.0\\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $p = 0.003$) then stabilizes ($34$--$36.5\\%$ for $N \\geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$); both the decline and plateau are well-powered (MDE ${\\sim}8$~pp at 80\\% power; Appendix~\\ref{app:dose-response}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Death rate vs.\\ trial count for Retry and Reflexion on 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$). Reflexion drops steeply through $N{=}3$ then plateaus; Retry is nearly flat. Horizontal lines: 72B ($30.6\\%$) and deterministic ($16.3\\%$) baselines. Error bands: Wilson 95\\% CIs.}\n\\label{fig:dose-response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Environment Boundary Probes}\n\\label{sec:math-contrast}\n\\label{sec:cross-env}\n\nWe probe two environments as boundary cases to test the framework's specificity (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal-reversal}). On MATH Level~4--5~\\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$), internal reasoning and code-interpreter accuracy are near-identical ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$): without a catastrophic failure mechanism, no routing signal emerges despite substantial task difficulty. ALFWorld~\\citep{alfworld-2021} (135 games, no death mechanism, DAF $= 0\\%$) shows 94.6\\% of non-tie labels favoring the information-advantaged strategy (which enumerates admissible actions); Llama-3.1-8B replicates at 100\\%. The framework diagnoses this qualitatively different mechanism---information asymmetry rather than death avoidance---without modification, confirming that DAF correctly attributes zero signal to death when none exists.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.95\\columnwidth]{figures/signal_reversal.pdf}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\caption{Cross-environment label distributions. ScienceWorld and ALFWorld both favor deterministic via different mechanisms (death avoidance vs.\\ information asymmetry); MATH shows no routing signal.}\n\\label{fig:signal-reversal}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Baselines}\n\\label{sec:baselines}\n\nAlways-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points (Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}). Random routing ($-22.38$) falls between the two pure strategies, confirming that the gap is not an artifact of evaluation methodology. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\\% of decisions matching always-deterministic---an \\emph{oracle degeneracy} where perfect per-episode foresight provides marginal benefit over the trivial always-deterministic baseline (Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold-routing}).\n\n\\subsection{Router Training: Inconclusive Results}\n\\label{sec:router-diagnostic}\n\nA Qwen3 LoRA router (0.6B--4B; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) trained on counterfactual labels shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$) across all model scales; episode-level evaluation yields $47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$, below majority-vote.\\footnote{Consistent with noisy-label bounds~\\citep{natarajan2013learning}: ${\\sim}$88\\% of step-level labels are uninformative with 90.5\\%/3.7\\% class imbalance (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}).} Leave-one-task-out degrades to chance ($49.2\\%$), confirming signal is a per-task-type constant rather than a learnable function of step-level observations. Deployed end-to-end, the best router (4B) scores $-10.70$, not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$); this negative result is consistent with the death-dominance finding, as death avoidance cannot be predicted from pre-episode features when it depends on multi-step trajectory dynamics.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & Score & 95\\% CI & Death \\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nAlways-Internal & $-39.07$ & [$-46.40$, $-32.27$] & 42.9 \\\\\nAlways-Deterministic & $-9.47$ & [$-14.71$, $-4.29$] & 16.3 \\\\\nRandom & $-22.38$ & [$-28.67$, $-15.75$] & 27.8 \\\\\nOracle & $-6.58$ & [$-11.54$, $-1.70$] & 14.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTrained Router$^\\dagger$ & $-10.70$ & [$-16.33$, $-5.27$] & 17.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Episode-weighted mean scores on ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes). $\\dagger$\\,Single seed (42); see Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness} for 8-seed robustness.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n",

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Large language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.
4	In ScienceWorld \citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone, as the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated), while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}
5	
6	Existing routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \citep{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \citep{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \citep{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026}, model tiers \citep{mtrouter-2026}, or episode-level model selection under sparse rewards \citep{budget-aware-routing-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. These approaches, which operate in non-catastrophic settings, share the assumption that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---an assumption that remains untested: no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing preferences in interactive environments.
7	
8	We propose a \emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding. Always-deterministic matches 96.3\% of oracle decisions, capping routing's marginal value at 2.89 points (\S\ref{sec:baselines}); removing death episodes reveals that oracle access provides almost no non-death advantage ($\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$, 9.6\% of total gap; \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}). Two independent 70B-class models yield DAF${}=92.4$--$101.0\%$ (Llama-70B exceeds 100\%: death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap), and Reflexion ($N{=}3$) similarly drives DAF above 100\% (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).
9	
10	This addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\% tie rate; \S\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\S\ref{sec:baselines}). Our approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \citep{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, comparing episode outcomes.
11	
12	Cross-context checks probe specificity: MATH Level~4--5 ($N{=}795$, code interpreter) shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$, $p = 0.81$); Llama-3.1-8B replication ($N{=}220$) confirms death dominance across model families (direction consistency 10/10; \S\ref{sec:cross-model}). Four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) reduce death rates but none closes the gap to deterministic safety, with ${\geq}$12~pp residual gaps (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).
13	An exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
14	
15	Our contributions: (1)~an \textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\% tie rate), decomposes routing signal via penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics, and provides build-vs-buy signal from a ${\sim}$50-episode pilot (Appendix~\ref{app:decision-framework}); (2)~\textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels} in ScienceWorld stem from death asymmetry---replicated across three 7--9B model families (DAF $88$--$90\%$), four interventions, $10{\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models (DAF $92.4$--$101.0\%$), and cross-environment probes (MATH, ALFWorld)---while a step-level router recovers only task-type classification (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\ref{sec:cross-model}).
16	
17	\begin{figure*}[t]
18	\centering
19	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}
20	\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}.}
21	\label{fig:pipeline}
22	\end{figure*}
23

    "newString": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nLarge language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \\emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.\nIn ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone, as the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated), while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \\emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}\n\nExisting routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \\citep{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \\citep{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\citep{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \\citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026}, model tiers \\citep{mtrouter-2026}, or episode-level model selection under sparse rewards \\citep{budget-aware-routing-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. These approaches, which operate in non-catastrophic settings, share the assumption that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---an assumption that remains untested: no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing preferences in interactive environments.\n\nWe propose a \\emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding. Always-deterministic matches 96.3\\% of oracle decisions, capping routing's marginal value at 2.89 points (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}); removing death episodes reveals that oracle access provides almost no non-death advantage ($\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$, 9.6\\% of total gap; \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}). Two independent 70B-class models yield DAF${}=92.4$--$101.0\\%$ (Llama-70B exceeds 100\\%: death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap), and Reflexion ($N{=}3$) similarly drives DAF above 100\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\n\nThis addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\\% tie rate; \\S\\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\\S\\ref{sec:baselines}). Our approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \\citep{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, comparing episode outcomes.\n\nCross-context checks probe specificity: MATH Level~4--5 ($N{=}795$, code interpreter) shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$); Llama-3.1-8B replication ($N{=}220$) confirms death dominance across model families (direction consistency 10/10; \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}). Four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) reduce death rates but none closes the gap to deterministic safety, with ${\\geq}$12~pp residual gaps (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).\nAn exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n\nOur contributions: (1)~an \\textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\\% tie rate), decomposes routing signal via penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics, and provides build-vs-buy signal from a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot (Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}); (2)~\\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels} in ScienceWorld stem from death asymmetry---replicated across three 7--9B model families (DAF $88$--$90\\%$), four interventions, $10{\\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models (DAF $92.4$--$101.0\\%$), and cross-environment probes (MATH, ALFWorld)---while a step-level router recovers only task-type classification (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\\ref{sec:cross-model}).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure*}\n",

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	\begin{figure*}[t]
4	\centering
5	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}
6	\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}.}
7	\label{fig:pipeline}
8	\end{figure*}
9	
10	Large language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.
11	In ScienceWorld \citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone, as the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated), while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}
12	
13	Existing routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \citep{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \citep{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \citep{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026}, model tiers \citep{mtrouter-2026}, or episode-level model selection under sparse rewards \citep{budget-aware-routing-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. These approaches, which operate in non-catastrophic settings, share the assumption that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---an assumption that remains untested: no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing preferences in interactive environments.
14	
15	We propose a \emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding. Always-deterministic matches 96.3\% of oracle decisions, capping routing's marginal value at 2.89 points (\S\ref{sec:baselines}); removing death episodes reveals that oracle access provides almost no non-death advantage ($\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$, 9.6\% of total gap; \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}). Two independent 70B-class models yield DAF${}=92.4$--$101.0\%$ (Llama-70B exceeds 100\%: death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap), and Reflexion ($N{=}3$) similarly drives DAF above 100\% (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).
16	
17	This addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\% tie rate; \S\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\S\ref{sec:baselines}). Our approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \citep{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, comparing episode outcomes.
18	
19	Cross-context checks probe specificity: MATH Level~4--5 ($N{=}795$, code interpreter) shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$, $p = 0.81$); Llama-3.1-8B replication ($N{=}220$) confirms death dominance across model families (direction consistency 10/10; \S\ref{sec:cross-model}). Four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) reduce death rates but none closes the gap to deterministic safety, with ${\geq}$12~pp residual gaps (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).
20	An exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
21	
22	Our contributions: (1)~an \textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\% tie rate), decomposes routing signal via penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics, and provides build-vs-buy signal from a ${\sim}$50-episode pilot (Appendix~\ref{app:decision-framework}); (2)~\textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels} in ScienceWorld stem from death asymmetry---replicated across three 7--9B model families (DAF $88$--$90\%$), four interventions, $10{\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models (DAF $92.4$--$101.0\%$), and cross-environment probes (MATH, ALFWorld)---while a step-level router recovers only task-type classification (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\ref{sec:cross-model}).
23	
24	\begin{figure*}[t]
25	\centering
26	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}
27	\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}.}
28	\label{fig:pipeline}
29	\end{figure*}
30	
```
